Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 9396 ms + 1383 ms + 32 ms = 10811 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 6716 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 17528 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 10811 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 10811 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 41738 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 54598 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 671 ms + 2 ms = 673 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 5 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 678 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 673 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 673 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 806 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 9159 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 29941 ms + 3133 ms + 27 ms = 33101 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 7435 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 40536 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 33101 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 33101 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 41738 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 54598 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 945 ms + 1 ms = 947 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 2 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 949 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 947 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 947 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 806 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 9159 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 17648 ms + 3039 ms + 136 ms = 20824 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 13497 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 34321 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 20824 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 20824 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 41738 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 54598 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 1372 ms + 4 ms = 1376 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 3 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 1379 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 1376 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 1376 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 806 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 9159 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 13183 ms + 0 ms + 175 ms = 13357 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 6062 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 19420 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 13357 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 13357 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 41738 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 54598 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset harness across served-from-pack assets = 0 ms + 0 ms + 1 ms = 1 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 1 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 2 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 1 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 1 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 806 ms
All observed BL asset wait sum = 9159 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + harness tax. The wall-clock time the asset was paused in our handler.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this URL, but the TokenPack run did not request this exact URL in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Legend
  • Baseline — no interception. Browser fetches assets natively. Closest to real production traffic.
  • Baseline-CDP — same harness rig as TP (intercept + fulfill via CDP) but bytes refetched Node-side. Lets us isolate harness IPC tax. Network behaviour differs from baseline (no H/2 multiplexing on Node fetches).
  • TokenPack — intercept + serve from packed PNG. Pays harness IPC tax on every served asset.
  • Δ TP vs BL (prod fairness) — TP minus baseline. Real comparison including harness handicap. If TP wins here, it wins despite the rig.
  • Δ TP vs BL-CDP (tech fairness) — TP minus baseline-CDP. Both pay harness tax, so this isolates pack-vs-direct. Tech-level win.
  • Est. prod TP (–harness) — projection of TP duration with harness IPC tax stripped out. Computed as TP × (1 − harnessRatio) where harnessRatio = sum(harness)/sum(totalMs) across all served-from-pack assets. Approximates a real Service-Worker deployment where the browser delivers pack bytes directly without CDP roundtrips. Estimate, not measurement.
